#10578. What Is the Best UI Testing Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner.

Prices are taken from each vendor's site or published comparisons as of October 2026. Check current pricing before you buy.

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

Argos is an open source visual testing platform with SDKs for Playwright, Storybook, Vitest, and Cypress. It adds cloud baselines and team review on top of the screenshots your tests already produce.

Strengths: Simple flat pricing. The Hobby plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month, and Pro is $100/month for 35,000 screenshots, with a budget cap available.

Trade-offs: Argos focuses on visual diffs. Argos compares screenshots captured by your own test runs, so browser coverage depends on what your CI runs, and it doesn't offer hosted mobile simulators. It also has a smaller ecosystem than the larger platforms.

Best for: Small teams that want hosted visual review at a predictable price.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic, Percy or Argos.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, Chromatic, Percy and Argos all offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10577. Graffiti artist tags another GTA 6 ad after Brooklyn mural backlash

The graffiti artist who tagged Rockstar’s giant GTA 6 mural in Brooklyn has struck again, tagging another GTA ad despite a wave of backlash from fans.

Known as Jelous, the artist first sprayed his tag across the hand-painted mural on Wythe Avenue and North 11th Street in Williamsburg, less than a month after it was completed on October 10.

A black and white “JELOUS” tag covered much of the bottom of the Brooklyn mural, including Lucia’s legs, though Jason, the Rockstar logo and the game’s title were left untouched.

After the backlash, Jelous hid the comments on his Instagram post, but that hasn’t stopped him. He has now tagged another GTA ad [LOCATION/DETAILS], and opened up about why he did it in an interview with Brick and Grit.

A post shared by Brick&Grit (@brickandgrit)

“I grew up playing GTA, so when I saw the ad, I knew I had to put my tag on it. It wasn’t planned. I saw the opportunity and just went for it,” he said.

He also hit back at fans upset by the tag. “People don’t understand graffiti culture. I didn’t go over a mural, I tagged an advertisement. What surprised me most was how personally people took it.”

“It’s ironic how GTA celebrates chaos and lawlessness, yet a little real-life vandalism gets everyone so upset,” he added.

Despite the backlash, Jelous said he was surprised by the attention. “I never imagined it would blow up like this, but it’s cool to see how much conversation a simple tag has started.”

GTA 6 fans split after spotting hint from supposed Rockstar dev

Comments on his second post are still open, and fans haven’t held back. “Using GTA 6 fame because he knows otherwise his account would be dead,” reads the most-liked comment, while another wrote, “Imagine thinking that those horrible letters look good lol.”

A post shared by jelous.ktd (@jelous.ktd)

Others backed him, though. “Imagine getting mad at a real graff writer doing graffiti on a billboard about a game that centers around violence and living the street life,” one wrote, while another joked, “GTA taught us crime, it’s only right.”

Some even thought Rockstar might be on board, with one fan commenting, “Lol bro GTA might actually like this. I think it’s great promo all around.”

Rockstar hasn’t commented on either tag, or said whether the Brooklyn mural will be repaired ahead of the game’s November 19 release on PS5 and Xbox Series X|S.

#10576. AI swarms could hijack democracy without anyone noticing

A new kind of political threat may be emerging, and it is far less visible than protests or traditional voter manipulation. Researchers warn that highly realistic AI-controlled personas could soon play a major role in shaping public opinion and influencing democratic systems.

A recent policy forum paper published in Science describes how large groups of AI-generated personas can convincingly imitate human behavior online. These systems can enter digital communities, participate in discussions, and influence viewpoints at extraordinary speed. Unlike earlier bot networks, these AI agents can coordinate instantly, respond to feedback, and maintain consistent narratives across thousands of accounts.

Rapid progress in large language models and multi-agent systems has made it possible for a single operator to manage vast networks of AI "voices." These personas can appear authentic, adopt local language and tone, and interact in ways that feel natural to other users.

They are also capable of running millions of small-scale experiments to determine which messages are most persuasive. This allows them to refine their communication strategies in real time and generate what appears to be widespread public agreement. In reality, that consensus is artificially created and designed to influence political discussions.

Deepfakes and fake news signal early risks

Although fully developed AI swarms are still largely theoretical, researchers say there are already warning signs. These include AI-generated deepfakes and fake news outlets that have influenced recent election conversations in countries such as the United States, Taiwan, Indonesia, and India, according to UBC computer scientist Dr. Kevin Leyton-Brown.

At the same time, monitoring organizations have identified pro-Kremlin networks spreading large volumes of online content. This activity is believed to be aimed at shaping the data used to train future AI systems, potentially influencing how those systems behave and what information they prioritize.

Experts warn of growing impact on democracy

Looking ahead, experts believe AI swarms could significantly affect the balance of power in democratic societies. Dr. Leyton-Brown cautioned that these systems are likely to change how people trust information online. "We shouldn't imagine that society will remain unchanged as these systems emerge. A likely result is decreased trust of unknown voices on social media, which could empower celebrities and make it harder for grassroots messages to break through."

Researchers suggest that upcoming elections may serve as a critical test for this technology. The key challenge will be recognizing and responding to these AI-driven influence campaigns before they become too widespread to control.

Materials provided by University of British Columbia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10574. Call of Duty Modern Warfare 4 review

It's easy to dislike Call of Duty. Aside from the annual cycle of development, marketing, and controversy that has defined the series identity for the past 22 years, the series has become an integral part of how video games prop up the military-industrial complex. Whilst some of the stories - especially 2025's Black Ops 7 - have deteriorated into the realms of science-fiction absurdity, many of the narratives you'll find in the Modern Warfare splinter of the series are more grounded in reality. When you're playing as semi-fictional UK and US troopers, taking part in illegal operations in foreign territory, wantonly executing people over differences in ideology, Call of Duty can feel gross: paper-thin propaganda designed to glorify war and reinforce lines between 'them' and 'us'.

But Modern Warfare 4 shies away from this. A little. The bar is low, granted, but there is a slightly more humanitarian slant to the game this year - aping 2009's No Russian, there are a few missions that plant you into the shoes of the 'enemy', and ask you to make moral choices that, I won't lie, I was rather shocked to see manifest in a CoD game. In 2019, developer Infinity Ward asked what would happen if there was a terrorist attack on the streets of London. In 2022, the studio ponders what happens when a co-ordinated international military operation goes awry on a Mexican oil rig. In 2023, we go to Lower Manhattan as the Russian army stages an attack on the New York Stock Exchange. So, in moving our lens and making us play as a North Korean militiaman, I was genuinely surprised to learn that - if you refuse your orders - you are summarily shot, leaving the rest of the mission to play out in front of your lifeless corpse. There is no 'game over' state for not complying: the horrors carried out by the ruthless regime carry on even in the face of your dereliction of duty. It might be using baby speak, by literary standards, but here I think Call of Duty is trying to make a point.

And it doesn't fall on ears unwilling to listen, either. Just this week, a South Korean politician urged the country's president to play Modern Warfare 4, so striking is its depiction of a theorised North Korean assault on the nation (which makes the dev's inclusion of real disaster footage an even more regrettable gaffe. Whilst it's important to remember that a country's population is not its leadership, the news has been full of North Korean threats and bluster for many years now. And in Modern Warfare 4, Infinity Ward wanted to tell a story "ripped from the headlines". The result is a slightly more 'reactive' setup, where a militarised nation responds to an invasion rather than instigates one, which in turn gives a sense of urgency and speculation that is more agreeable than, say, another shadowy skirmish in the Middle East, or an insurgency into some proxy state secretly controlled by Russia (don't hold your breath).

As we flip back and forth between ex-SAS hard bastard, John Price (who we've been following since 2007) and a ragtag bunch of conscribed South Korean soldiers and US Marine allies that find themselves in the middle of a brutal, casualty-heavy North Korean invasion, we get to see Call of Duty campaign design at its finest. There's a rousing John Wick-inspired shootout in a club that evolves into a parkour dash across Manhattan rooftops. There's a cinematic and genuinely impressive shooting gallery-like encounter in a trainyard that evokes classic arcade lightgun design. There's an expansive open world area that toys with stealth and sniping, there's a car chase through the streets of Paris, and there's a beachhead assault that ties it all together nicely.

Beyond even those, the standout mission is Stranded, which sees the South Korean team deep in enemy territory, fighting for their lives. This introduces crafting for a few missions - something that could feel tacked-on and rote, but is actually deployed like a lever by Infinity Ward to wedge open just how important each of the various tools at your disposal are. Want a thermal scope? Then you're going to prioritise that over more munitions. Want explosives? Then your basic arms will suffer. It serves to emphasise just how well every aspect of Modern Warfare's toolkit complements every other piece, resulting in this rich, deep, interlocking marvel that makes older Call of Duty games look rudimentary in comparison.

This is complemented by setpieces like Traffic, where you engage on a breakneck chase through the streets of Paris in a romp sure to cost the Parisian treasury millions in collateral damage. Here, you get to see the breadth and depth of the enemy AI that Infinity Ward has crafted - which no doubt went hand-in-hand with the new DMZ mode, more on that later - as we witness just how complex the studio's engine can get: destruction, cover mechanics, and so many different kinds of visual effect all rush on-screen at once. It's overwhelming, it's intimidating, it's electrifying. It's peak Call of Duty.

Between these standout missions, there are moments where you're asked to consider what it's like for the pawns on the board - the other soldiers, the unnamed, those whose strings are being pulled by forces they don't understand. It's a bit Fisher-Price, a bit A-Level, a 'War is Bad' pin badge worn on a jacket from an Army Surplus store, but hey: I'd rather this than a completely unobserved 'ooh-rah!' story about the US army coming to the rescue again.

The downside of the campaign comes more from its setup and pacing than its content, then. Price's story wraps up without applause, really - I won't ruin the specifics here, but his actions and what he does to quash the machinations of a very oddly-implemented Mads Mikkelsen are sort of left in the dark to rot, as the desperate resistance of our South Korean recruits takes the spotlight. Again, I think Call of Duty might be trying to say something here: that the morally ambiguous and collateral damage-accumulating actions of one desperate operative that actually saved the world don't get to breathe in the daylight; instead, the 'courageous' defense of privates on the frontline should be lauded. War is messy, no-one is in the right, everyone loses except for the multinationals putting the hardware in the hands of the humans. Is that what I'm supposed to come out of this thinking?

Whatever the emotional impact is, you can't argue with the physical one: this is a tight, eight-hour campaign with moments up there with Modern Warfare's best. It stops before it ever risks getting overlong, it's got plenty of new ideas to throw at you and - played on the hardest mode - it's a tight puzzle box of encounter design that's just as obsessed with detail as it is with Michael Bay-esque spectacle. It's a far cry from whatever the hell was going on in Black Ops 7 last year.

Reeling from the fact I think I nearly felt something during a Call of Duty campaign, I needed to boot up the multiplayer - a mode which has by now been refined into its near-perfect form, an ultra-fast, turbo-injected mosh pit of quick scopes, slide cancels, and kill corridors. Map design is great - of course it is, it's Infinity Ward - and the gunplay is good as it ever has been in the Modern Warfare games. Since 2019, when Infinity Ward went to great expense to rebuild its engine, the Call of Duty experience has been luxurious. The sound design alone is worthy of applause, every clack of a bolt-action lever, every shell casing falling to the floor, every fizz of thermite boring through your cover… it's immediately identifiable and extremely important. To play Modern Warfare 4 without a decent set of headphones or a good surround sound setup is to intentionally handicap yourself.

There are a few qualms, as ever: some respawn mechanics are vexing (especially on quicker, gnarlier maps - this week's addition of Shipment is a hilariously bad example of this), and some of the weapon balancing has yet to be totally calmed down, in part owing to a last-minute change to weapon damage that extends the time-to-kill beyond Infinity Ward's usual fare. Whilst this means there's a bit of chaos for early adopters like myself, it actually speaks to the level of nuance Infinity Ward has to play with: I'd love to see the backend of Modern Warfare 4, with all its knobs and dials, seeing how the studio tweaks and balances guns to keep up with what players are doing. The slightly lower time-to-kill is actually a fascinating experiment in figuring out what Modern Warfare is beyond its selection of guns - and I'd happily take a bit of weird balancing a deeper, more intricate damage system.

Speaking of, the customisation system also works nicely here. With the right attachments, some guns are elevated from 'useful' to 'essential'. The ISO Nightshade SMG seems a bit busted right now, and I keep getting chewed up by suspiciously powerful one-two taps of the Type 73 LMG. I'm a born sharpshooter, and the Oris 8.6 is an early favourite for popping heads across the map, especially dressed up with a 2.1x scope with thermal imagine. The overall economy of guns and range-versus-power feels a bit off as it stands - and is quite literally being addressed as I type - but I'm playing multiplayer in launch week, so there's always going to be some friction.

Then there's Kill Block: an experimental and dynamic rotating multiplayer map and mode concept. It's absurd in its pitch: by using a selection of 'slabs', it can form 500+ unique combinations of map, meaning no match plays out quite like the last. Maps in Call of Duty are (in)famously defined by their sight lines; if you stand here, you can shoot someone there. If you camp this corner, you'll rack up kills. If you snipe on this lane, you'll collect greenhorn scalps a-plenty. Kill Block turns all that on its head, forcing players to be more reactive, and engage more with the systems on offer in Modern Warfare 4, rather than rely on instincts baked in from two-plus decades of CoD. With the promise of more modular elements to come - from fan-favourite maps like Crash, for instance - I can see this mode and setup becoming yet another staple in the ever-expanding Call of Duty canon. This is how you breathe life into the more competitive-focused modes that often fail to shine alongside respawn-focused experiences like Team Deathmatch.

I have to praise the complexity and wizardry Infinity Ward has displayed with how the guns handle; each attachment is meaningful, most guns feel unique, and the feedback you get from firing any armament is exquisite. It has all the production value of a dedicated military sim, but in action it's readable and almost arcade-like in its simplicity. No one does it like Infinity Ward, and even though this feels like an iteration on the last three Modern Warfare games, I won't knock the developers down for staying in their lane and developing a formula that works.

And that brings us to DMZ. Initially, I was cynical. 'This is Call of Duty chasing the bag', I thought. 'This is trend-chasing gone awry'. But Infinity Ward is not afraid of change, and I shouldn't be either. The extraction shooter trend shows no signs of abating, and with even Call of Duty managing to make it work within the specific arcade-cum-sim setup of its engine, I need to accept that. I don't think it's a stretch to say DMZ could be a standalone game - packed with enough to go toe-to-toe with the likes of Marathon or Arc Raiders.

Simplified controls, motion sensor functionality, deadzone/trigger customisation, vibration/trigger effects, reduce tinnitus sound, audio focus (PS5 only), mono audio options, HUD customisation, motion blur/DoF customisation, colour filters, camera movement reduction options, aim assist, aiming sensitivity, movement/sprint assists, FoV sliders.

The Hajin city map, smartly repurposed from the campaign, is expansive and packed with nuance, and the tasks you're given within the city limits are quick and painless - more than I can say of Marathon. You can easily and somewhat permanently get cash that persists even if you die, you can sacrifice max health to 'revive' from a down-but-not-out state, and you get XP for doing just about anything. The result is an extraction shooter experience that feels more casual and agreeable to the average FPS enjoyer; this isn't a 'lifestyle game' like other extraction shooters. Even if it does have its own pseudo-stealth system, for some reason.

In many ways, DMZ emulates what Activision did with Warzone: a wrangling of a complicated formula that makes sense within the uber-specific Call of Duty ecosystem. It's a great interpretation of the PvPvE formula that's been slowly but surely evolving over the past decade or so, and I think this is where DMZ finds its niche: Arc Raiders has become more about the PvE side of things (at least in my experience) and Marathon and Tarkov go hard on the PvP. DMZ somehow treads the line between both and, as time goes on and Infinity Ward monitors the mode and how people engage with it, I imagine its niche will only become more distinct compared to its genre stable-mates. We're already seeing some changes bleed into the game, and it's not even been out a week - it bodes well for the future.

Modern Warfare 4 represents the highest tide for Call of Duty in seven years. Infinity Ward needed to pull off a balancing act I don't envy; injecting just enough nostalgia into a series propped up with so many conventions and expectations there's barely room for anything new, alongside enough innovation and intrigue to keep people interested. Beyond that, after several weaker years in a row, Black Ops 7 in particular caused enough reputation damage that even the most loyal CoD fanatics started to see through the series' aging veneer. And so, even if the campaign occasionally struggles under the weight of legacy, and the multiplayer is happy to keep rolling on the same wheels Modern Warfare has been using for four games now, Infinity Ward has succeeded - after four years away from the helm - in saving Call of Duty. Modern Warfare 4 is a course correction sorely needed, and deftly deployed.

A copy of the game was provided for this review by Activision Blizzard.

#10572. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.

#10571. 8-bit horror Tenebris Somnia mixes weird live-action horror with a retro 2D adventure to impressive and unsettling effect

I've been captivated by the idea of wrong-footing an audience ever since playing Faith: The Unholy Trinity. That game presents like an 8-bit horror, then proceeds to bend the rules of what an 8-bit game can do - or perhaps what you remember they could do - in order to surprise and scare you. Tenebris Somnia is very similar. Indeed, it's executive produced by the developer of Faith, Airdorf Games, so the connection runs even deeper. Tenebris Somnia utilises the same retro aesthetic with its grinding chiptune music, its pixelated graphics, and old-school presentation. But the difference is it takes the rule-bending a step further, essentially welding two very different experiences together: a retro-styled 2D adventure and a full-on live-action film. And it's horrific, in all the right ways.

To begin with, Tenebris Somnia is a slow, plodding, fairly unremarkable affair that plays a bit like a point-and-click adventure. You're a young woman on her way to her former boyfriend's apartment, checking it out because you suspect something has happened there. You move from screen to screen, examining points of interest as you pass them, collecting odds and ends - an empty bottle, a mysterious book - and storing them in your inventory for later. Eventually, there's a locked door, and you'll have to piece together the scattered clues in order to unlock it. And despite the apparent urgency of the situation - the carnage you find in your ex-boyfriend's apartment - the pace is slow; deliberately old-school in its approach. Walk back and forth, examine everything, try "combining" items in your inventory, "use" them on the environment. Did it work? No? Try something else.

But this is where Tenebris Somnia begins to distinguish itself: your busywork is often rewarded with a glossy live-action sequence - clips of real actors in impressive costume and make-up enacting key moments from the unfolding adventure. A person with what appears to be two faces, one inverted, rushes out of the darkness toward you. A strange woman suddenly appears in a lift, offering you a gift. Two waxen figures embrace each other in bed. A giant lady with a hole in her face stretches towards the ceiling. It's captivatingly weird and very well-produced, finished with a level of polish that's impressive in an otherwise lo-fi indie experience such as this. And there's a reason for this: the live-action scenes are made by filmmaker Andrés Borghi, who's worked on a number of films including acclaimed Argentinian horror When Evil Lurks. Borghi has also made games of his own, so there's a natural overlap and understanding there.

It's these live-action sequences that stand Tenebris Somnia apart, that bring another, unusual dimension to it. That pixelated creature rushing toward you in the hallway: it has more substance in your mind now you've seen it up close. That enigmatic lady in a cocktail dress: you know the contours of her face and the strange demonic symbols branded there. You know the world better, more intimately now, and that gives it more impact. These live-action moments also create a kind of propulsive anticipation, knowing that when you do solve a puzzle or unlock that mysterious door, lavish reward and revelation awaits you there.

But I have my doubts about the game overall. Granted, I'm only early on, so perhaps I'm yet to really settle in. But I find its plodding stop-start rhythm works against the suspense and horror. Laboriousness deflates the tension. Frequently revisiting areas robs them of their surprises. And I haven't found much inherent interest in exploring new areas of the game; the writing feels functional to me rather than intriguing, and that's a problem, because in the adventure game sequences it has to do so much of the heavy lifting.

But I remain hopeful. I've watched a little more of Tenebris Somnia than I've managed to play, and the movie-making story at its heart - one focusing on a horror short of your ex's creation - appears to deepen and circle back on itself in interesting ways. Everything you do somehow seems tied to that movie - around deals with devilish entities and the grotesque consequences they bring. That filmic motif repeats again and again. And who knows? Perhaps the live-action sequences represent more than the things our character is experiencing. So there's intrigue there, depth, thought, layers, and, by the looks of it, plenty of imagination to support it.

#10570. Viral retro gaming creator Jacob Payne dies as family warns fans of impersonators

Viral retro gaming creator Jacob Payne, better known as Retro Jake, has died, his family announced on Instagram on November 7.

His family said on Instagram that Payne was “in heaven now” and had “fought until the very end,” while asking followers to keep his loved ones in their thoughts and prayers as they take time to mourn.

Payne’s death came after he had been largely absent from regular uploads. His last listed YouTube video, titled “Life Update. Long Video Incoming,” was posted on October 25, while a local report said his family had announced he was hospitalized with severe heart failure the following week. His family has not publicly provided a formal cause of death.

The Retro Jake account later issued a warning after the family said scammers were already attempting to exploit the situation. Followers were told not to send money or answer questions from anyone claiming to represent Payne, his relatives or his friends.

The family said all legitimate information would be shared through Retro Jake’s official Facebook page. It also warned that no financial support would be requested unless it was announced through that account.

Payne was known for buying, selling and trading retro games while showcasing collections and customer interactions at his stores. He also supported local causes, including a “Console For Kids” initiative with the Richmond Police Department that delivered video game systems to Girls Inc. last Christmas.

#10569. What we've been playing - "I've been intrigued by horrible images of live-action sequences for weeks"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Happy Halloween! Are you scared? I am: I'm in a theatre play that opens next week and we're not ready yet. I'm also off to a ghost stories evening at the theatre tonight; I've had a few people there tell me, with faces as straight as a ruler, of their eerie encounters. Actually, one encounter involved me.

I was in a two-hander there, a play with only two people in it, and I got to the changing room one evening to see a concerned look on the face of the other person in the show. "Have you only just got here?" he asked. "Yes," I said in response. And at this, he blanched. He's a gruff sort of fellow with a streetwise sensibility: not a person I would associate with being easily scared. But he was. "I was just talking to you," he said. "You made the curtain move as you walked past." I remember the hairs on my arms and neck standing up, and I haven't been as confident sitting in that dressing room alone since.

But back to games! This, as you know, is our regular feature where we talk about what we've been playing this week. This week, Marie cooks with Death; Connor revisits a game that terrified him as a teenager; and Bertie is a little bit disappointed by a new indie horror.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago (it happens). And now onward!

My supernatural farm is coming together very slowly, and I do mean slowly. I'm not in a rush this time - my many restarts of Stardew Valley have taught me to be patient while building or I'll want to wipe everything and start afresh. Also, I do not do scary games in the slightest, so this is my version of a halloween-themed entry, and I am fine with that.

So: my current farm contains a small livestock barn, a vegetable patch, a rather fetching vineyard next to some kegs, and I've just started to renovate the lower level by the beach. What that will be is yet to be decided, but the slow progress isn't bothering me as much as I thought it would. In fact, being unable to race through it is making it more enjoyable.

Also, watching Death learning to cook was not on my 2026 bingo card, but I am adamant a friendship will form from the literal ashes of his creations.

Given we're in the Halloween season and the site has been diving into all things scary, I wanted to pop back and play the game that kicked off a horror revival of sorts when I was almost a teenager: Amnesia!

Like many my age, my first exposure to Amnesia was through YouTube playthroughs back in the Let's Play early years. I didn't actually touch it myself until years later, dipping my toes in the game and swiftly pulling them back out, frightened.

Now I'm older and hardened by adult life, I wanted to give it another go and see how horror games have evolved over time. While they certainly have come a way since 2010, Amnesia holds up! It's well worth playing if you want a blast from the past.

I'm a bit disappointed. I've been intrigued by horrible images of live-action sequences from Tenebris Somnia for weeks, anticipating what I believed would be a terrifying experience. But a couple of hours into the game, I haven't had that. I've written about this at more length in a Tenebris Somnia preview, but suffice to say the adventure game half of Tenebris Somnia lets it down a bit, I think. However, I'm still impressed; I'm impressed by people approaching horror - and games - in these interesting ways.

It also reminds me strongly of Faith: The Unholy Trilogy, which is another, closely related 8-bit horror game that really is terrifying. It's tighter and meaner, and its crazed, hand-drawn MS Paint cinematics are worth seeing up close for yourself.

What a spooky week it's been! After Monday's trawl into some of our favourite scary games, I delved - some 30 years after my original encounter - back into pioneering survival horror Ecstatica, dongs and all. Since then, we've had post-Soviet grimness, minty fresh breath and misery, plus Bertie straddling the line between retro pixel art and retro FMV.

In amongst all that (and a bit of Silver Pines), I've squeaked in some time with The Killing Stone. It's very early days, but I'm already intrigued by its distinctive mix of roguelike deck-builder and first-person narrative horror, especially given the folkloric aspects of it all as you, a Scandinavian witch's apprentice, reckon with previous dealings with the devil.

It's fascinating so far, with an unusual card-based combat system that plays out across a single line, meaning placement matters as you attempt to snuff out your opponent's orb at the far end by battling through - or around - its guards. Each of your units can be augmented with spells and abilities placed strategically, and adjacently, outside of the battlefield, introducing even more possibilities. And I especially love the fact you can play the whole thing in modern or ye olde English, the strong voice cast making fine work of both.

I'm excited to explore more, then, but with Halloween here I've other engagements to see through first. Two annual movie traditions: Trick 'r Treat, and then - as someone old enough to have been scarred by it the first time around - controversial BBC classic Ghostwatch, with Michael Parkinson, Craig Charles, and Sarah Greene. Over three decades later it remains remarkably effective, and that bedroom Pipes reveal still haunts me.

#10568. Generating Web-Ready Images With AI: Formats, Sizes, and srcset Done Right

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming an important part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product visuals, blog illustrations, backgrounds, and other digital content. However, creating an image is only the first step. Preparing it for efficient web delivery is equally important.

Unoptimized images can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay page rendering. These problems can affect Core Web Vitals, especially Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques, including image resizing, modern formats, Node.js automation, responsive delivery, and performance testing.

Images often represent a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. When developers upload large images without processing them, visitors may experience slower loading times, particularly on mobile networks.

Large image files that take longer to download.

Incorrect dimensions for different screen sizes.

Missing image dimensions that cause layout shifts.

Excessive compression that reduces visual quality.

Unnecessary downloads caused by poor responsive configuration.

A consistent optimization workflow helps address these problems while maintaining the intended visual appearance.

Before preparing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width hero banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small article illustration.

These are suggested dimensions, not fixed requirements. The actual size depends on the website layout and the devices being supported.

When preparing source visuals, developers can compare different AI image tools to understand how image generation workflows handle aspect ratios, output dimensions, and visual details.

Regardless of the generation method, keep an original high-quality version. This master file can be used to create smaller versions without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Choosing an appropriate image format can reduce file size while maintaining acceptable visual quality.

AVIF: Provides efficient compression for many photographs and detailed illustrations.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and environments requiring broad compatibility.

PNG: Suitable for screenshots, lossless graphics, and images that require transparency.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare the resulting file sizes and inspect image quality before selecting a format.

For technical details, consult the MDN Web Docs guide to image file formats.

Manually resizing multiple images can become time-consuming. Automation provides a consistent way to generate different image dimensions and formats.

Sharp is a Node.js library that supports resizing, compression, and image conversion.

The script generates WebP and AVIF versions at several widths.

The quality values are starting points. Adjust them after checking the output for compression artifacts, loss of detail, and unnecessary file size.

For additional configuration options, refer to the official Sharp documentation.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on available resources and screen layout.

The HTML srcset attribute provides multiple image candidates, while sizes describes the expected rendered width.

The browser uses the available image candidates and layout information to select a suitable resource.

Make sure the sizes attribute accurately reflects the actual website layout. Incorrect values can cause browsers to download larger images than necessary.

Responsive delivery is particularly useful when the same visual appears in different layouts across desktop, tablet, and mobile devices.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide alternative image formats with a fallback.

The browser selects a supported source, while the img element provides fallback behavior and alternative text.

Always verify that the referenced files exist and that their dimensions match the intended layout.

Images without defined dimensions can cause page content to move while resources are loading. This can negatively affect Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Include width and height attributes whenever possible:

The browser can use the dimensions to reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

Images below the initial viewport can also use native lazy loading:

However, avoid lazy-loading the main hero image when it contributes to LCP. Important above-the-fold images should be loaded promptly.

Reducing file size should not significantly affect the visual details required by the website.

This is particularly important for images containing:

A practical quality-checking process includes:

Compare the optimized image with its original version.

Inspect the image at its actual display dimensions.

Test the output on mobile and desktop screens.

The smallest file is not necessarily the best option. A slightly larger image may provide noticeably better visual quality.

After implementing the optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Open Chrome DevTools and select the Network panel. Filter requests by images and reload the webpage.

Mobile devices receive appropriately sized images.

The browser downloads unnecessary image files.

Modern formats are being served correctly.

Important images load without unnecessary delays.

Image dimensions match their display requirements.

You can inspect the image source selected by the browser using:

For websites containing multiple images, inspect each relevant element.

Finally, run an audit using Google PageSpeed Insights. It can help identify oversized images and other performance issues.

The web.dev guide to optimizing LCP also provides useful technical guidance for diagnosing loading delays.

Remember that image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, and JavaScript execution can also influence page loading.

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one image format always provides the best compression.

Using incorrect responsive image settings.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Ignoring the actual image selected by the browser.

Measuring file size without checking visual quality.

A proper testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

The fundamental optimization techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, generated visuals may contain complex textures and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design, but efficient delivery requires more than simply exporting and uploading a visual.

Choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive delivery. Include image dimensions to prevent layout shifts, prioritize important visual elements, and test the results across different devices.

By following these practices, developers can maintain visual quality, reduce unnecessary downloads, and create faster, more reliable website experiences.

#10566. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#10565. Battlefield 6 player sues EA after permanent ban and wins to get account back

A Battlefield 6 player says he took EA to court after being permanently banned from the shooter and won, with the judge ordering the publisher to reverse the sanction and restore access to his account.

Reddit user Rychadv shared the update in r/Battlefield on November 8, claiming EA could not support the reason for his ban.

Rychadv’s case began with a post in r/electronicarts. He said he had returned to Battlefield 6 after a long break to check out Season 4 and was greeted by a ban notification when he launched the game. He denied using cheats, exploits or inappropriate chat, and said his appeal produced no useful explanation beyond three dots.

The player later wrote that EA told him the ban was for cheating, prompting him to say, “I’d like to see them prove that.” He then said he filed a lawsuit in Brazil and submitted evidence from Windows showing that no illegal software or unauthorized devices had been used on his PC.

Rychadv said he used Brazil’s Juizado Especial Cível, a lower-complexity civil court process similar to small claims court.

He said the filing came with no court fees or taxes and that he represented himself because he is a lawyer. Brazilian court guidance describes these proceedings as covering lower-complexity civil and consumer disputes, with cases generally free to file.

He also claimed EA appeared in the case and submitted a defense, but made several claims without evidence to support them. According to Rychadv, the ruling restored his account, ordered compensation and imposed a fine on EA. He did not disclose the amount of either payment.

In follow-up comments, Rychadv confirmed that EA had already unbanned him and that his account was “back to normal.” He also said the legal process cost him nothing, while EA’s total payment was roughly four times the price of the game, plus the fine

Battlefield 6 devs respond after players hit with missing content bug

Battlefield 6 player gets Steam refund after 470 hours because their favorite mode was removed

Battlefield 2042 players call for EA boycott after failed refund petition

EA’s public rules add context to the dispute. The publisher says Battlefield 6 uses its Javelin anti-cheat system and lists third-party software, exploits and other unfair advantages as violations. EA also says it requires clear evidence of cheating to ban a player.

Its account support page directs players to the Penalty History tool to review penalties and submit appeals, with removing a penalty listed as a possible outcome when a mistake is found.

The public Reddit and X posts do not include a case number or a link to a court docket. No EA response appears in the posts reviewed for this story, so the court result is currently based on Rychadv’s account and follow-up comments.

#10563. Silver Pines is a great survival horror throwback with an unexpected Metroidvania twist, but it makes me glad the genre's moved on

It's those bloody typewriters all over again. Developer Wych Elm Games' Silver Pines is fantastic in a whole bunch of ways, from its woozy small-town ambience to its mix of taut survival horror and exploratory Metroidvania. But from the very first moment I saw its save system, its please-insert-coin phone box demands, I knew our relationship was going to be a fractious one.

Silver Pines begins, however, in the gloom of a 1950s diner; lights off, windows boarded, as the titular town awaits an oncoming storm. You are private investigator Red Walker, but your jittery demeanour and Valium drawl suggests you're equally oblivious to the whos, whats, and whys of it all. Out in the parking lot, a phone box rings; there's cryptic talk of a missing musician and a motel room. Then, the scene set, it's onward into the heart of the town.

Silver Pines dabbles in neo-noir and psychological horror, but its debt to David Lynch - Twin Peaks in particular - is unmistakable. If the 50s diner opener wasn't enough of a tip-off, it's not long before the town's cast of beautifully rotoscoped oddballs begin making themselves known; enigmatic conversations unfurling in awkward non-sequiters. Then later: as Red passes through a suspicious door, reality seems to bend entirely, taking him to a dingy nightclub bar somewhere out of time. There's no talking giant, but we do get a song.

Twin Peaks' iconography has been borrowed and repackaged so much in the 36 years since its landmark arrival, it's hard to pay it homage without ringing the creative death knell. So it's testament to Silver Pines that, for all its hat-tips, it still manages to find a mood that feels like its own. Granted, its one that exists at the intersection David Lynch and Silent Hill (itself indebted to Twin Peaks) but the town's ambience - a kind of dishevelled melancholy - is an affecting one. Its world, built from layers upon layers of strikingly painterly 2D artwork, is drab and desolate; perpetually dank and lost in artfully controlled shadow. Its sorrowful monstrosities shimmer like a suggestion. And beneath the wind and the rain and the wailing of lost souls lies an ambient synthline so slight the soundscape skirts close to ASMR.

That, though, is entirely fitting for an adventure whose pace is such a languid one; as woozily unfocused as Red seems to be when he first picks up that phone. That's not a criticism though. Silver Pines isn't afraid to create space for its insidious ambience to slither in. There are pockets of staccato violence, absolutely, but between the deliberately stilted gunplay and awkwardly stumbling melee its primary rhythms are exploratory ones.

Which is where the Metroidvania stylings come in. Silver Pines, the town, is a vast, interconnected space: drizzly exteriors lead to gloomy interiors that branch out to new pathways or double back to previously impassable ones (you'll be hearing Red muttering "The door opens from the other side!" in your dreams). Sometimes you'll stumble across a perfectory puzzle, but progress most often requires identifying which part of the map, perhaps a location first seen much earlier, might succumb to new tools: a boxcutter that opens up passageways sealed with plastic; a pistol that can shatter rusty padlocks. And a shout-out to one of my favourite underused Metroidvania mechanics - the ability to photograph points of interest and pin them to your map, serving as reminders for future you.

It all makes for a haltingly ambulatory flow that suits and serves the quieter mood. But truthfully, having hit the five-hour mark, I'm finding the whole thing a bit of a struggle as I grapple with its intentional throwback design. Beneath Silver Pines' sumptuously bleak presentation lies an ancient heart. And while I'm certainly not criticising a deliberately old-school adventure for playing it old-school, it is making me thankful the genre's moved on.

Silver Pines, like most survival horror, builds tension through scarcity and impermanence, offering thrilling glimpses of power only to take them all away again: bullets are limited, melee weapons break, and the resources needed to restore them are equally few. Without all that, your only hope of survival is a stumbling duck-and-weave. But Silver Pines spins a particularly punishing web, tying everything to the finite number of coins left out in the world. Ammo, duct tape, health items, and so on are rare finds on the map, but comparatively plentiful in vending machines. However, coins are also the currency you'll need for phone box saves, meaning every choice you make feels even more perilous; the perpetual threat of a death spiral.

And the truth is, as much as I love survival horror, I've always hated limited saves; not just the push-your-luck stresses they invoke as I'm forced to decide if I dare risk - for instance - my last Resident Evil typewriter ribbon, but the steadily deflating atmosphere when my luck runs out and I've got to do the same section all over again. It's a problem modern survival horror still grapples with, of course, but, by jettisoning the arbitrary restrictions of limited save currency, they're able to manage a more sustained momentum.

And by binding its survival systems so tightly - the threat of weapons failure, the threat of death - under the guise of coins, Silver Pines has made me even more obsessive about my saves, even more cautious about frivolous spending. And it's turned mood into mechanisms. I'm min-max route plotting, enemy tracking, bullet counting, and limiting my box cutter swings - so much energy spent on mental spreadsheet-building I'm no longer immersed in the mystery of the thing. And the open-ended structure does rather exacerbate the issue: its long, slow stretches of nothing followed by unexpected deaths make redoing bits particularly wearying.

Of course, it's not Silver Pines' fault it's igniting my old-school survival horror PTSD. And I'm fully aware there are sickos out there who love this kind of thing; the queasily unshakeable threat of spiralling disaster and the harsh punishment when calculations are off and disaster finally comes. But you know what? I certainly respect Silver Pines, even if we don't always get along. It's a gorgeously woozy exercise in mood, with a fishing line mystery that - for all my grumbles - just keeps pulling me on. Not for nothing is its enigmatic bar known as The Rabbit Hole, and as the search for Eddie Velvet continues, here I am; lost in the warrens again.

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10562. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).
